But after a few minutes, my eyes are still moving over the page, but my mind has just completely wandered off. I end up having to go back and read the same part all over again because I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Does this problem with following conversations and reading happen all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't exactly kept track of it like that. I mean... I mostly just notice it when it becomes a problem, like when I have to ask someone to repeat themselves or when I lose my place on the page. But as for whether it happens every single time, or if there are times it doesn't happen at all... I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or have to re-read something?\n\nPatient: Not really anxious, no. I mean, I don't get upset about it. It's mostly just... a little annoying, I guess, having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to read a page over again. But it doesn't make me nervous or worried. I just sort of deal with it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches or ear problems when you have trouble following conversations or reading?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any headaches or ear trouble when it happens. Physically, I feel fine. It's just my focus that wanders off, not any kind of pain or hearing problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to tasks or activities outside of conversations and reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean... I can't think of anything else right off hand. It mostly just seems to be when I'm trying to read or when somebody is talking to me. Like, at the museum, people will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking right at them. But then I realize I completely missed the middle of what they just said. I always end up having to ask them to run it by me again. I always end up having to ask them to run it by me again. It happens enough that I figured I should probably come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when people are talking to you, or do you also have trouble following along when you're reading something?\n\nPatient: It happens when I'm reading, too. I can sit down with a gallery packet or, you know, a set of notes, and I'll start out fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes are still moving over the page, but my mind has just completely wandered off. I end up having to go back and read the same part all over again because I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Does this problem with following conversations and reading happen all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't exactly kept track of it like that. I mean... I mostly just notice it when it becomes a problem, like when I have to ask someone to repeat themselves or when I lose my place on the page. But as for whether it happens every single time, or if there are times it doesn't happen at all... I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or have to re-read something?\n\nPatient: Not really anxious, no. I mean, I don't get upset about it. It's mostly just... a little annoying, I guess, having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to read a page over again. But it doesn't make me nervous or worried. I just sort of deal with it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches or ear problems when you have trouble following conversations or reading?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any headaches or ear trouble when it happens. Physically, I feel fine. It's just my focus that wanders off, not any kind of pain or hearing problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to tasks or activities outside of conversations and reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean... I can't think of anything else right off hand. It mostly just seems to be when I'm trying to read or when somebody is talking to me. I mean, I'm not reading things I don't want to read, and the conversations are usually just with my wife or friends about normal stuff. So it's not that I'm bored with it, really. It's just... I don't know, my mind just drifts off anyway, even if it's something I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wanders off more often when the topic is complex or detailed?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed that, to be honest. It just happens regardless.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this problem with your mind drifting off, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's pretty much always been this way. Even way back in grade school, I remember my report cards would say things like, uh, \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" It was actually to the point where my mother used to have to sit right beside me while I did my homework just to keep me on track. She had to do that until I was about twelve or so. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any difficulties with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or, you know, do things without thinking. I'm actually a pretty quiet guy. Sitting still has never really been a problem for me... it's mostly just the spacing out that I deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind drifting off is related to feeling stressed, overwhelmed, or emotionally upset in any way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's related to stress or feeling upset. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. I always end up having to ask them to run it by me again. It happens enough that I figured I should probably come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when people are talking to you, or do you also have trouble following along when you're reading something?\n\nPatient: It happens when I'm reading, too. I can sit down with a gallery packet or, you know, a set of notes, and I'll start out fine. I end up having to go back and read the same part all over again because I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Does this problem with following conversations and reading happen all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't exactly kept track of it like that. I mean... I mostly just notice it when it becomes a problem, like when I have to ask someone to repeat themselves or when I lose my place on the page. But as for whether it happens every single time, or if there are times it doesn't happen at all... I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or have to re-read something?\n\nPatient: Not really anxious, no. But it doesn't make me nervous or worried. Physically, I feel fine. It's just my focus that wanders off, not any kind of pain or hearing problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to tasks or activities outside of conversations and reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean... I can't think of anything else right off hand. It mostly just seems to be when I'm trying to read or when somebody is talking to me. It doesn't seem to matter if it's a complicated topic or just... a simple chat about the groceries. My mind just kind of drifts off either way. So, no, I wouldn't say the details make it any worse. It just happens regardless.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this problem with your mind drifting off, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's pretty much always been this way. Even way back in grade school, I remember my report cards would say things like, uh, \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" It was actually to the point where my mother used to have to sit right beside me while I did my homework just to keep me on track. She had to do that until I was about twelve or so. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any difficulties with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or, you know, do things without thinking. I'm actually a pretty quiet guy. Sitting still has never really been a problem for me... it's mostly just the spacing out that I deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind drifting off is related to feeling stressed, overwhelmed, or emotionally upset in any way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's related to stress or feeling upset. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. It doesn't seem tied to my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations and reading affecting your work or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I volunteer at a local museum, and they've had to, well, quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me because I just can't keep track of all the details in the meetings. And I used to lead the art walks, but I've stopped feeling confident doing that the way I used to. It's just getting too hard to keep the information straight when people are talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations affecting your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. My wife is pretty used to me, so if I lose track of what she's saying, she just tells me again. It hasn't caused any big arguments or anything like that. And with my friends... no, I haven't really noticed any problems there either. It seems to be fine on that front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. It doesn't seem tied to my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations and reading affecting your work or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I volunteer at a local museum, and they've had to, well, quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me because I just can't keep track of all the details in the meetings. And I used to lead the art walks, but I've stopped feeling confident doing that the way I used to. It's just getting too hard to keep the information straight when people are talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations affecting your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. My wife is pretty used to me, so if I lose track of what she's saying, she just tells me again. It hasn't caused any big arguments or anything like that. And with my friends... no, I haven't really noticed any problems there either. It seems to be fine on that front.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to learn new things or pick up new skills because of your mind drifting off?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I haven't really tried to learn any big new skills lately anyway, but when I do need to figure something out, I manage okay. It's more just the day-to-day conversations and reading where I notice the drifting, not so much when I'm actively trying to learn how to do something new.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks. Well, what brings me in is... I've been noticing this problem when people talk to me. Like, at the museum, people will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking right at them. But then I realize I completely missed the middle of what they just said. I always end up having to ask them to run it by me again. It happens enough that I figured I should probably come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when people are talking to you, or do you also have trouble following along when you're reading something?\n\nPatient: It happens when I'm reading, too. I can sit down with a gallery packet or, you know, a set of notes, and I'll start out fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes are still moving over the page, but my mind has just completely wandered off. I end up having to go back and read the same part all over again because I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Does this problem with following conversations and reading happen all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't exactly kept track of it like that. I mean... I mostly just notice it when it becomes a problem, like when I have to ask someone to repeat themselves or when I lose my place on the page. But as for whether it happens every single time, or if there are times it doesn't happen at all... I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or have to re-read something?\n\nPatient: Not really anxious, no. I mean, I don't get upset about it. It's mostly just... a little annoying, I guess, having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to read a page over again. But it doesn't make me nervous or worried. I just sort of deal with it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches or ear problems when you have trouble following conversations or reading?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any headaches or ear trouble when it happens. Physically, I feel fine. It's just my focus that wanders off, not any kind of pain or hearing problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to tasks or activities outside of conversations and reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean... I can't think of anything else right off hand. It mostly just seems to be when I'm trying to read or when somebody is talking to me. If I'm just doing normal things around the house or running errands, I haven't really noticed any problems paying attention to that. I guess it's just the reading and the listening, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find the topics you're reading about or the conversations you're having interesting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, mostly. I mean, I'm not reading things I don't want to read, and the conversations are usually just with my wife or friends about normal stuff. So it's not that I'm bored with it, really. It's just... I don't know, my mind just drifts off anyway, even if it's something I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wanders off more often when the topic is complex or detailed?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed that, to be honest. It doesn't seem to matter if it's a complicated topic or just... a simple chat about the groceries. My mind just kind of drifts off either way. So, no, I wouldn't say the details make it any worse. It just happens regardless.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this problem with your mind drifting off, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's pretty much always been this way. Even way back in grade school, I remember my report cards would say things like, uh, \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" It was actually to the point where my mother used to have to sit right beside me while I did my homework just to keep me on track. She had to do that until I was about twelve or so. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any difficulties with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or, you know, do things without thinking. I'm actually a pretty quiet guy. Sitting still has never really been a problem for me... it's mostly just the spacing out that I deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind drifting off is related to feeling stressed, overwhelmed, or emotionally upset in any way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's related to stress or feeling upset. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. It doesn't seem tied to my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations and reading affecting your work or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I volunteer at a local museum, and they've had to, well, quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me because I just can't keep track of all the details in the meetings. And I used to lead the art walks, but I've stopped feeling confident doing that the way I used to. It's just getting too hard to keep the information straight when people are talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations affecting your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. My wife is pretty used to me, so if I lose track of what she's saying, she just tells me again. It hasn't caused any big arguments or anything like that. And with my friends... no, I haven't really noticed any problems there either. It seems to be fine on that front.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to learn new things or pick up new skills because of your mind drifting off?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I haven't really tried to learn any big new skills lately anyway, but when I do need to figure something out, I manage okay. It's more just the day-to-day conversations and reading where I notice the drifting, not so much when I'm actively trying to learn how to do something new.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any special accommodations or support for learning or schoolwork?\n\nPatient: No, I never did. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF88, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks. Well, what brings me in is... I've been noticing this problem when people talk to me. Like, at the museum, people will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking right at them. But then I realize I completely missed the middle of what they just said. I always end up having to ask them to run it by me again. It happens enough that I figured I should probably come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when people are talking to you, or do you also have trouble following along when you're reading something?\n\nPatient: It happens when I'm reading, too. I can sit down with a gallery packet or, you know, a set of notes, and I'll start out fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes are still moving over the page, but my mind has just completely wandered off. I end up having to go back and read the same part all over again because I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Does this problem with following conversations and reading happen all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't exactly kept track of it like that. I mean... I mostly just notice it when it becomes a problem, like when I have to ask someone to repeat themselves or when I lose my place on the page. But as for whether it happens every single time, or if there are times it doesn't happen at all... I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or have to re-read something?\n\nPatient: Not really anxious, no. I mean, I don't get upset about it. It's mostly just... a little annoying, I guess, having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to read a page over again. But it doesn't make me nervous or worried. I just sort of deal with it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches or ear problems when you have trouble following conversations or reading?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any headaches or ear trouble when it happens. Physically, I feel fine. It's just my focus that wanders off, not any kind of pain or hearing problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to tasks or activities outside of conversations and reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean... I can't think of anything else right off hand. It mostly just seems to be when I'm trying to read or when somebody is talking to me. If I'm just doing normal things around the house or running errands, I haven't really noticed any problems paying attention to that. I guess it's just the reading and the listening, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find the topics you're reading about or the conversations you're having interesting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, mostly. I mean, I'm not reading things I don't want to read, and the conversations are usually just with my wife or friends about normal stuff. So it's not that I'm bored with it, really. It's just... I don't know, my mind just drifts off anyway, even if it's something I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wanders off more often when the topic is complex or detailed?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed that, to be honest. It doesn't seem to matter if it's a complicated topic or just... a simple chat about the groceries. My mind just kind of drifts off either way. So, no, I wouldn't say the details make it any worse. It just happens regardless.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this problem with your mind drifting off, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's pretty much always been this way. Even way back in grade school, I remember my report cards would say things like, uh, \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" It was actually to the point where my mother used to have to sit right beside me while I did my homework just to keep me on track. She had to do that until I was about twelve or so. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any difficulties with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or, you know, do things without thinking. I'm actually a pretty quiet guy. Sitting still has never really been a problem for me... it's mostly just the spacing out that I deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind drifting off is related to feeling stressed, overwhelmed, or emotionally upset in any way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's related to stress or feeling upset. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. It doesn't seem tied to my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations and reading affecting your work or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I volunteer at a local museum, and they've had to, well, quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me because I just can't keep track of all the details in the meetings. And I used to lead the art walks, but I've stopped feeling confident doing that the way I used to. It's just getting too hard to keep the information straight when people are talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations affecting your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. My wife is pretty used to me, so if I lose track of what she's saying, she just tells me again. It hasn't caused any big arguments or anything like that. And with my friends... no, I haven't really noticed any problems there either. It seems to be fine on that front.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to learn new things or pick up new skills because of your mind drifting off?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I haven't really tried to learn any big new skills lately anyway, but when I do need to figure something out, I manage okay. It's more just the day-to-day conversations and reading where I notice the drifting, not so much when I'm actively trying to learn how to do something new.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any special accommodations or support for learning or schoolwork?\n\nPatient: No, I never did. School was a long time ago for me, obviously, but... no, I didn't get any extra help or special arrangements back then. I just went to regular classes and got through high school without anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your mind drifting off in quiet environments, like when you're alone, as much as you do in noisy or busy places?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a difference between the two. It just sort of happens whether I'm sitting quietly at home reading or if there's a lot going on around me. So, yeah, I guess it's about the same either way. I haven't really paid much attention to the background noise when it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF88, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks. Well, what brings me in is... I've been noticing this problem when people talk to me. Like, at the museum, people will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking right at them. But then I realize I completely missed the middle of what they just said. I always end up having to ask them to run it by me again. It happens enough that I figured I should probably come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when people are talking to you, or do you also have trouble following along when you're reading something?\n\nPatient: It happens when I'm reading, too. I can sit down with a gallery packet or, you know, a set of notes, and I'll start out fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes are still moving over the page, but my mind has just completely wandered off. I end up having to go back and read the same part all over again because I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Does this problem with following conversations and reading happen all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't exactly kept track of it like that. I mean... I mostly just notice it when it becomes a problem, like when I have to ask someone to repeat themselves or when I lose my place on the page. But as for whether it happens every single time, or if there are times it doesn't happen at all... I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or have to re-read something?\n\nPatient: Not really anxious, no. I mean, I don't get upset about it. It's mostly just... a little annoying, I guess, having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to read a page over again. But it doesn't make me nervous or worried. I just sort of deal with it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches or ear problems when you have trouble following conversations or reading?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any headaches or ear trouble when it happens. Physically, I feel fine. It's just my focus that wanders off, not any kind of pain or hearing problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to tasks or activities outside of conversations and reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean... I can't think of anything else right off hand. It mostly just seems to be when I'm trying to read or when somebody is talking to me. If I'm just doing normal things around the house or running errands, I haven't really noticed any problems paying attention to that. I guess it's just the reading and the listening, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find the topics you're reading about or the conversations you're having interesting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, mostly. I mean, I'm not reading things I don't want to read, and the conversations are usually just with my wife or friends about normal stuff. So it's not that I'm bored with it, really. It's just... I don't know, my mind just drifts off anyway, even if it's something I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wanders off more often when the topic is complex or detailed?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed that, to be honest. It doesn't seem to matter if it's a complicated topic or just... a simple chat about the groceries. My mind just kind of drifts off either way. So, no, I wouldn't say the details make it any worse. It just happens regardless.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this problem with your mind drifting off, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's pretty much always been this way. Even way back in grade school, I remember my report cards would say things like, uh, \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" It was actually to the point where my mother used to have to sit right beside me while I did my homework just to keep me on track. She had to do that until I was about twelve or so. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any difficulties with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or, you know, do things without thinking. I'm actually a pretty quiet guy. Sitting still has never really been a problem for me... it's mostly just the spacing out that I deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind drifting off is related to feeling stressed, overwhelmed, or emotionally upset in any way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's related to stress or feeling upset. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. It doesn't seem tied to my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations and reading affecting your work or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I volunteer at a local museum, and they've had to, well, quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me because I just can't keep track of all the details in the meetings. And I used to lead the art walks, but I've stopped feeling confident doing that the way I used to. It's just getting too hard to keep the information straight when people are talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations affecting your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. My wife is pretty used to me, so if I lose track of what she's saying, she just tells me again. It hasn't caused any big arguments or anything like that. And with my friends... no, I haven't really noticed any problems there either. It seems to be fine on that front.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to learn new things or pick up new skills because of your mind drifting off?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I haven't really tried to learn any big new skills lately anyway, but when I do need to figure something out, I manage okay. It's more just the day-to-day conversations and reading where I notice the drifting, not so much when I'm actively trying to learn how to do something new.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any special accommodations or support for learning or schoolwork?\n\nPatient: No, I never did. So, yeah, I guess it's about the same either way. I haven't really paid much attention to the background noise when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have any difficulties in school with specific subjects, such as reading or mathematics?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I got through high school fine. I wasn't at the top of the class or anything, but I didn't struggle with math or reading specifically.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF88, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks. Well, what brings me in is... I've been noticing this problem when people talk to me. Like, at the museum, people will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking right at them. But then I realize I completely missed the middle of what they just said. I always end up having to ask them to run it by me again. It happens enough that I figured I should probably come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when people are talking to you, or do you also have trouble following along when you're reading something?\n\nPatient: It happens when I'm reading, too. I can sit down with a gallery packet or, you know, a set of notes, and I'll start out fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes are still moving over the page, but my mind has just completely wandered off. I end up having to go back and read the same part all over again because I didn't actually take any of it in.\n\nDoctor: Does this problem with following conversations and reading happen all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't exactly kept track of it like that. I mean... I mostly just notice it when it becomes a problem, like when I have to ask someone to repeat themselves or when I lose my place on the page. But as for whether it happens every single time, or if there are times it doesn't happen at all... I really couldn't say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or have to re-read something?\n\nPatient: Not really anxious, no. I mean, I don't get upset about it. It's mostly just... a little annoying, I guess, having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to read a page over again. But it doesn't make me nervous or worried. I just sort of deal with it and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches or ear problems when you have trouble following conversations or reading?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any headaches or ear trouble when it happens. Physically, I feel fine. It's just my focus that wanders off, not any kind of pain or hearing problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to tasks or activities outside of conversations and reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean... I can't think of anything else right off hand. It mostly just seems to be when I'm trying to read or when somebody is talking to me. If I'm just doing normal things around the house or running errands, I haven't really noticed any problems paying attention to that. I guess it's just the reading and the listening, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find the topics you're reading about or the conversations you're having interesting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, mostly. I mean, I'm not reading things I don't want to read, and the conversations are usually just with my wife or friends about normal stuff. So it's not that I'm bored with it, really. It's just... I don't know, my mind just drifts off anyway, even if it's something I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind wanders off more often when the topic is complex or detailed?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed that, to be honest. It doesn't seem to matter if it's a complicated topic or just... a simple chat about the groceries. My mind just kind of drifts off either way. So, no, I wouldn't say the details make it any worse. It just happens regardless.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this problem with your mind drifting off, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's pretty much always been this way. Even way back in grade school, I remember my report cards would say things like, uh, \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" It was actually to the point where my mother used to have to sit right beside me while I did my homework just to keep me on track. She had to do that until I was about twelve or so. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any difficulties with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or, you know, do things without thinking. I'm actually a pretty quiet guy. Sitting still has never really been a problem for me... it's mostly just the spacing out that I deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind drifting off is related to feeling stressed, overwhelmed, or emotionally upset in any way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's related to stress or feeling upset. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. It doesn't seem tied to my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations and reading affecting your work or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I volunteer at a local museum, and they've had to, well, quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me because I just can't keep track of all the details in the meetings. And I used to lead the art walks, but I've stopped feeling confident doing that the way I used to. It's just getting too hard to keep the information straight when people are talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations affecting your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. My wife is pretty used to me, so if I lose track of what she's saying, she just tells me again. It hasn't caused any big arguments or anything like that. And with my friends... no, I haven't really noticed any problems there either. It seems to be fine on that front.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to learn new things or pick up new skills because of your mind drifting off?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I haven't really tried to learn any big new skills lately anyway, but when I do need to figure something out, I manage okay. It's more just the day-to-day conversations and reading where I notice the drifting, not so much when I'm actively trying to learn how to do something new.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any special accommodations or support for learning or schoolwork?\n\nPatient: No, I never did. School was a long time ago for me, obviously, but... no, I didn't get any extra help or special arrangements back then. I just went to regular classes and got through high school without anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your mind drifting off in quiet environments, like when you're alone, as much as you do in noisy or busy places?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a difference between the two. It just sort of happens whether I'm sitting quietly at home reading or if there's a lot going on around me. So, yeah, I guess it's about the same either way. I haven't really paid much attention to the background noise when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have any difficulties in school with specific subjects, such as reading or mathematics?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I got through high school fine. I wasn't at the top of the class or anything, but I didn't struggle with math or reading specifically. I just did the work and got by.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with a learning disorder, such as dyslexia or a disorder of written expression?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Like I said, I just kind of got through school without any special help or anything. Nobody ever mentioned dyslexia or any other learning disorder to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It just happens regardless.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this problem with your mind drifting off, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's pretty much always been this way. Even way back in grade school, I remember my report cards would say things like, uh, \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" It was actually to the point where my mother used to have to sit right beside me while I did my homework just to keep me on track. She had to do that until I was about twelve or so. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any difficulties with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or, you know, do things without thinking. I'm actually a pretty quiet guy. Sitting still has never really been a problem for me... it's mostly just the spacing out that I deal with.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind drifting off is related to feeling stressed, overwhelmed, or emotionally upset in any way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's related to stress or feeling upset. Like I said, it happens when I'm just doing normal, everyday things. I could be perfectly calm, not a worry in the world, and my mind will still just wander off. It doesn't seem tied to my mood at all.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations and reading affecting your work or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is affecting things. I volunteer at a local museum, and they've had to, well, quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me because I just can't keep track of all the details in the meetings. And I used to lead the art walks, but I've stopped feeling confident doing that the way I used to. It's just getting too hard to keep the information straight when people are talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your problems with following conversations affecting your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. My wife is pretty used to me, so if I lose track of what she's saying, she just tells me again. It hasn't caused any big arguments or anything like that. And with my friends... no, I haven't really noticed any problems there either. It seems to be fine on that front.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to learn new things or pick up new skills because of your mind drifting off?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I haven't really tried to learn any big new skills lately anyway, but when I do need to figure something out, I manage okay. It's more just the day-to-day conversations and reading where I notice the drifting, not so much when I'm actively trying to learn how to do something new.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any special accommodations or support for learning or schoolwork?\n\nPatient: No, I never did. School was a long time ago for me, obviously, but... no, I didn't get any extra help or special arrangements back then. I just went to regular classes and got through high school without anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your mind drifting off in quiet environments, like when you're alone, as much as you do in noisy or busy places?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a difference between the two. It just sort of happens whether I'm sitting quietly at home reading or if there's a lot going on around me. So, yeah, I guess it's about the same either way. I haven't really paid much attention to the background noise when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have any difficulties in school with specific subjects, such as reading or mathematics?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I got through high school fine. I wasn't at the top of the class or anything, but I didn't struggle with math or reading specifically. I just did the work and got by.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with a learning disorder, such as dyslexia or a disorder of written expression?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Like I said, I just kind of got through school without any special help or anything. Nobody ever mentioned dyslexia or any other learning disorder to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated for attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. This is actually the first time I'm really talking to a doctor about this kind of thing. It never really came up before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
